| [[characters/lady-macbeth\|LADY MACBETH]] | (rising)<br>Sit, worthy friends. My lord is often thus,<br>And hath been from his youth. Pray you, keep seat.<br>The fit is momentary. Upon a thought<br>He will again be [[motifs/well\|well]]. If much you note him<br>You shall offend him, and extend his passion.<br>Feed, and regard him not.<br>She speaks apart with [[characters/macbeth\|Macbeth]]<br>Are you a man? |
| [[characters/macbeth\|MACBETH]] | Ay, and a bold one, that dare look on that<br>Which might appal the devil. |
| [[characters/lady-macbeth\|LADY MACBETH]] | O proper stuff!<br>This is the very painting of your [[concepts/fear\|fear]];<br>This is the air-drawn dagger which you said<br>Led you to [[characters/duncan\|Duncan]]. O, these flaws and starts,<br>Impostors to true [[concepts/fear\|fear]], would [[motifs/well\|well]] become<br>A woman’s story at a [[motifs/winter\|winter]]’s [[motifs/fire\|fire]]<br>Authorized by her grandam. Shame itself,<br>Why do you make such faces? When all’s done<br>You look but on a stool. |
| [[characters/macbeth\|MACBETH]] | Prithee see there. Behold, look, lo-how say you?<br>Why, what care I? If thou canst nod, speak, too!<br>If charnel-houses and our graves must send<br>Those that we bury back, our monuments<br>Shall be the maws of kites. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit [[archetypes/ghost\|Ghost]] |
| [[characters/lady-macbeth\|LADY MACBETH]] | What, quite unmanned in folly? |
| [[characters/macbeth\|MACBETH]] | If I stand here, I saw him. |
| [[characters/lady-macbeth\|LADY MACBETH]] | Fie, for shame! |
| [[characters/macbeth\|MACBETH]] | [[motifs/blood\|Blood]] hath been shed ere now, i’th’ olden [[motifs/time\|time]],<br>Ere human statute purged the gentle weal;<br>Ay, and since, too, murders have been performed<br>Too terrible for the ear. The [[motifs/time\|time]] has been<br>That, when the brains were out, the man would die,<br>And there an end. But now they rise again<br>With twenty mortal murders on their crowns,<br>And push us from our stools. This is more strange<br>Than such a murder is. |
| [[characters/lady-macbeth\|LADY MACBETH]] | (aloud)<br>My worthy lord,<br>Your noble friends do lack you. |
| [[characters/macbeth\|MACBETH]] | I do forget.<br>Do not muse at me, my most worthy friends.<br>I have a strange infirmity which is nothing<br>To those that know me. Come, [[motifs/love\|love]] and health to all,<br>Then I’ll sit down.<br>(To an attendant) Give me some wine. Fill full. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[archetypes/ghost\|Ghost]] |
| [[characters/macbeth\|MACBETH]] | I drink to th’ general joy of th’whole table,<br>And to our dear friend [[characters/banquo\|Banquo]], whom we miss.<br>Would he were here. To all and him we thirst,<br>And all to all. |
| LORDS | Our duties, and the pledge.<br>They drink |
| [[characters/macbeth\|MACBETH]] | (seeing the [[archetypes/ghost\|Ghost]])<br>Avaunt, and quit my sight! Let the earth hide thee.<br>Thy bones are marrowless, thy [[motifs/blood\|blood]] is cold.<br>Thou hast no speculation in those eyes<br>Which thou dost glare with. |
| [[characters/lady-macbeth\|LADY MACBETH]] | Think of this, good peers,<br>But as a thing of custom. ’Tis no other;<br>Only it spoils the pleasure of the [[motifs/time\|time]]. |
| [[characters/macbeth\|MACBETH]] | What man dare, I dare.<br>Approach thou like the rugged Russian bear,<br>The armed rhinoceros, or th‘Hyrcan tiger;<br>Take any shape but that, and my firm nerves<br>Shall never tremble. Or be alive again,<br>And dare me to the [[motifs/desert\|desert]] with thy sword.<br>If trembling I inhabit then, protest me<br>The baby of a girl. Hence, horrible [[archetypes/shadow\|shadow]],<br>Unreal mock’ry, hence! |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit [[archetypes/ghost\|Ghost]] |
| [[characters/macbeth\|MACBETH]] | Why so, being gone,<br>I am a man again. Pray you sit still. |
| [[characters/lady-macbeth\|LADY MACBETH]] | You have displaced the mirth, broke the good meeting<br>With most admired disorder. |
| [[characters/macbeth\|MACBETH]] | Can such things be<br>And overcome us like a [[motifs/summer\|summer]]’s cloud,<br>Without our special [[concepts/wonder\|wonder]]? You make me strange<br>Even to the disposition that I owe,<br>When now I think you can behold such sights<br>And keep the natural ruby of your cheeks<br>When mine is blanched with [[concepts/fear\|fear]].<br>Ross<br>What sights, my lord? |
| [[characters/lady-macbeth\|LADY MACBETH]] | I pray you, speak not. He grows worse and worse. 116<br>Question enrages him. At once, good [[motifs/night\|night]].<br>Stand not upon the order of your going,<br>But go at once. |
| LENNOX | Good [[motifs/night\|night]], and better health<br>Attend his majesty. |
| [[characters/lady-macbeth\|LADY MACBETH]] | A kind good-[[motifs/night\|night]] to all. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt Lords |
| [[characters/macbeth\|MACBETH]] | It will have [[motifs/blood\|blood]], they say. [[motifs/blood\|Blood]] will have [[motifs/blood\|blood]].<br>Stones have been known to move, and trees to speak,<br>Augurs and understood relations have<br>By maggot-pies and choughs and rooks brought forth<br>The secret’st man of [[motifs/blood\|blood]]. What is the [[motifs/night\|night]]? |
| [[characters/lady-macbeth\|LADY MACBETH]] | Almost at odds with morning, which is which. |
| [[characters/macbeth\|MACBETH]] | How sayst thou that [[characters/macduff\|Macduff]] denies his person<br>At our great bidding? |
| [[characters/lady-macbeth\|LADY MACBETH]] | Did you send to him, sir? |
| [[characters/macbeth\|MACBETH]] | I hear it by the way, but I will send.<br>There’s not a one of them but in his house<br>I keep a servant fee’d. I will tomorrow,<br>And betimes I will, to the weird sisters.<br>More shall they speak, for now I am bent to know<br>By the worst means the worst. For mine own good<br>All causes shall give way. I am in [[motifs/blood\|blood]]<br>Stepped in so far that, should I wade no more,<br>Returning were as tedious as go o’er.<br>Strange things I have in head that will to hand,<br>Which must be acted ere they may be scanned. |
| [[characters/lady-macbeth\|LADY MACBETH]] | You lack the season of all natures, [[motifs/sleep\|sleep]]. |
| [[characters/macbeth\|MACBETH]] | Come, we’ll to [[motifs/sleep\|sleep]]. My strange and [[concepts/self\|self]]-abuse<br>Is the initiate [[concepts/fear\|fear]] that wants hard use.<br>We are yet but young in deed. Exeunt |

### Atto 3, Scena 5

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Thunder. Enter the [[characters/three-witches\|three Witches]] meeting Hecate |
| FIRST [[characters/witch\|WITCH]] | Why, how now, Hecate? You look angerly. |
| HECATE | Have I not reason, beldams as you are?<br>Saucy and over-bold, how did you dare<br>To trade and traffic with [[characters/macbeth\|Macbeth]]<br>In riddles and affairs of [[motifs/death\|death]],<br>And I, the mistress of your charms,<br>The close contriver of all harms,<br>Was never called to bear my part<br>Or show the glory of our art?—<br>And, which is worse, all you have done 10<br>Hath been but for a wayward son,<br>Spiteful and wrathful, who, as others do,<br>Loves for his own ends, not for you.<br>But make amends now. Get you gone,<br>And at the pit of Acheron<br>Meet me i‘th’ morning. Thither he<br>Will come to know his destiny.<br>Your vessels and your spells provide,<br>Your charms and everything beside.<br>I am for th’air. This [[motifs/night\|night]] I’ll spend<br>Unto a dismal and a fatal end.<br>Great business must be wrought ere [[motifs/noon\|noon]].<br>Upon the corner of the [[motifs/moon\|moon]]<br>There hangs a vap‘rous drop profound.<br>I’ll catch it ere it come to ground,<br>And that, distilled by magic sleights,<br>Shall raise such artificial sprites<br>As by the strength of their illusion<br>Shall draw him on to his confusion.<br>He shall spurn fate, scorn [[motifs/death\|death]], and bear<br>His hopes ’bove wisdom, grace, and [[concepts/fear\|fear]];<br>And you all know security<br>Is mortals’ chiefest enemy. |
| SPIRITS | (singing dispersedly within)<br>Come away, come away.<br>Hecate, Hecate, come away. |
| HECATE | Hark, I am called! My little spirit, see,<br>Sits in a foggy cloud and stays for me.<br>The [[works/song-(poe)\|Song]] |
| *(didascalia)* | SPIRITS within |
| HECATE | Come away, come away,<br>Hecate, Hecate, come away. |
| HECATE | I come, I come, I come, I come,<br>With all the [[characters/speed\|speed]] I may,<br>With all the [[characters/speed\|speed]] I may.<br>Where’s Stadlin? |
| *(didascalia)* | SPIRIT within |
| HECATE | Here. |
| HECATE | Where’s Puckle? |
| *(didascalia)* | ANOTHER SPIRIT within |
| HECATE | Here. |
| *(didascalia)* | OTHER SPIRITS within |
| HECATE | And Hoppo, too, and Hellwain, too,<br>We lack but you, we lack but you.<br>Come away, make up the count. |
| HECATE | I will but ’noint, and then I mount. |
| *(didascalia)* | Spirits appear above. A Spirit like a Cat descends |
| *(didascalia)* | SPIRITS above |
| HECATE | There’s one comes down to fetch his dues,<br>A kiss, a coll, a sip of [[motifs/blood\|blood]],<br>And why thou stay’st so long I muse, I muse,<br>Since the air’s so sweet and good. |
| HECATE | O, art thou come? What news, what news? |
| SPIRIT LIKE | A CAT<br>All goes still to our delight. Either come, or else refuse, refuse. |
| HECATE | Now I am furnished for the flight.<br>She ascends with the spirit and sings<br><br>Now I go, now I fly,<br>Malkin my sweet spirit and I. |
| *(didascalia)* | SPIRITS and HECATE |
| HECATE | O what a dainty pleasure ‘tis<br>To ride in the air<br>When the [[motifs/moon\|moon]] shines fair,<br>And sing, and [[motifs/dance\|dance]], and toy, and kiss.<br>Over woods, high rocks and [[settings/mountains\|mountains]],<br>Over seas and misty fountains,<br>Over steeples, towers and turrets,<br>We fly by [[motifs/night\|night]] ’mongst troops of spirits.<br>No ring of [[motifs/bells\|bells]] to our ears sounds,<br>No howls of wolves, no yelps of hounds.<br>No, not the noise of waters-breach<br>Or cannons’ throat our height can reach. |
| *(didascalia)* | SPIRITS abovel |
| HECATE | No ring of [[motifs/bells\|bells]] to our ears sounds,<br>No howls of wolves, no yelps of hounds.<br>No, not the noise of waters-breach<br>Or cannons’ throat our height can reach. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt into the heavens the |
| HECATE | Spirit like a Cat and Hecate |
| FIRST [[characters/witch\|WITCH]] | Come, let’s make haste. She’ll soon be back again. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt |

### Atto 3, Scena 6

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Lennox and another Lord |
| LENNOX | My former speeches have but hit your thoughts,<br>Which can interpret farther. Only I say<br>Things have been strangely borne. The gracious<br>[[characters/duncan\|Duncan]]<br>Was pitied of [[characters/macbeth\|Macbeth]]: marry, he was dead;<br>And the right valiant [[characters/banquo\|Banquo]] walked too late,<br>Whom you may say, if’t please you, Fleance killed,<br>For Fleance fled: men must not walk too late.<br>Who cannot want the thought how monstrous<br>It was for [[characters/malcolm\|Malcolm]] and for Donalbain<br>To kill their gracious father? Damned fact,<br>How it did grieve [[characters/macbeth\|Macbeth]]! Did he not straight<br>In pious rage the two delinquents tear,<br>That were the slaves of drink, and thralls of [[motifs/sleep\|sleep]]?<br>Was not that nobly done? Ay, and wisely too,<br>For ‘twould have angered any heart alive<br>To hear the men deny’t. So that I say<br>He has borne all things [[motifs/well\|well]], and I do think<br>That had he [[characters/duncan\|Duncan]]’s sons under his key—<br>As, an’t please heaven, he shall not—they should find<br>What ’twere to kill a father. So should Fleance.<br>But peace, for from broad words, and ’cause he failed<br>His presence at the [[archetypes/tyrant\|tyrant]]’s feast, I hear<br>[[characters/macduff\|Macduff]] lives in disgrace. Sir, can you tell<br>Where he bestows himself? |
| LORD | The son of [[characters/duncan\|Duncan]]<br>From whom this [[archetypes/tyrant\|tyrant]] holds the due of birth<br>Lives in the English [[settings/court\|court]], and is received<br>Of the most pious Edward with such grace<br>That the malevolence of fortune nothing<br>Takes from his high respect. Thither [[characters/macduff\|Macduff]]<br>Is gone to pray the holy [[archetypes/king\|King]] upon his aid<br>To wake Northumberland and warlike Siward,<br>That by the help of these—with Him above<br>To ratify the work—we may again<br>Give to our tables meat, [[motifs/sleep\|sleep]] to our nights,<br>Free from our feasts and banquets bloody knives,<br>Do faithful homage, and receive free honours,<br>All which we pine for now. And this report<br>Hath so exasperate their [[archetypes/king\|king]] that he<br>Prepares for some attempt of war. |
| LENNOX | Sent he to [[characters/macduff\|Macduff]]? |
| LORD | He did, and with an absolute ‘Sir, not I,’<br>The cloudy messenger turns me his back<br>And hums, as who should say ‘You’ll rue the [[motifs/time\|time]]<br>That clogs me with this answer.’ |
| LENNOX | And that [[motifs/well\|well]] might<br>Advise him to a caution t’hold what,distance<br>His wisdom can provide. Some holy angel<br>Fly to the [[settings/court\|court]] of England and unfold<br>His message ere he come, that a swift blessing<br>May soon return to this our [[concepts/suffering\|suffering]] country<br>Under a hand accursed. |
| LORD | I’ll send my prayers with him. Exeunt |

### Atto 4, Scena 1

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | A Cauldron. Thunder. Enter the [[characters/three-witches\|three Witches]] |
| FIRST [[characters/witch\|WITCH]] | Thrice the brinded cat hath mewed. |
| SECOND [[characters/witch\|WITCH]] | Thrice, and once the hedge-pig whined. |
| THIRD [[characters/witch\|WITCH]] | Harpier cries “Tis [[motifs/time\|time]], ‘tis [[motifs/time\|time]].’ |
| FIRST [[characters/witch\|WITCH]] | Round about the cauldron go,<br>In the poisoned entrails throw.<br>Toad that under cold stone<br>Days and nights has thirty-one<br>Sweltered venom sleeping got,<br>Boil thou first i’th’ charmed pot. |
| ALL | [[topoi/double\|Double]], [[topoi/double\|double]], toil and trouble,<br>[[motifs/fire\|Fire]] burn, and cauldron bubble. |
| SECOND [[characters/witch\|WITCH]] | Fillet of a fenny snake,<br>In the cauldron boil and bake.<br>Eye of newt and toe of frog,<br>Wool of bat and tongue of dog,<br>Adder’s fork and blind-worm’s sting,<br>Lizard’s leg and owlet’s wing,<br>For a charm of powerful trouble,<br>Like a hell-broth boil and bubble. |
| ALL | [[topoi/double\|Double]], [[topoi/double\|double]], toil and trouble,<br>[[motifs/fire\|Fire]] burn, and cauldron bubble. |
| THIRD [[characters/witch\|WITCH]] | Scale of dragon, tooth of wolf,<br>Witches’ mummy, maw and gulf<br>Of the ravined salt-sea shark,<br>Root of hemlock digged i‘th’ dark,<br>Liver of blaspheming Jew,<br>Gall of goat, and slips of yew<br>Slivered in the [[motifs/moon\|moon]]’s eclipse,<br>Nose of Turk, and Tartar’s lips,<br>Finger of birth-strangled babe<br>Ditch-delivered by a drab,<br>Make the gruel thick and slab.<br>Add thereto a tiger’s chaudron<br>For th’ingredience of our cauldron. |
| ALL | [[topoi/double\|Double]], [[topoi/double\|double]], toil and trouble,<br>[[motifs/fire\|Fire]] burn, and cauldron bubble. |
| SECOND [[characters/witch\|WITCH]] | Cool it with a baboon’s [[motifs/blood\|blood]],<br>Then the charm is firm and good. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Hecate and the other [[characters/three-witches\|three Witches]] |
| HECATE | O, [[motifs/well\|well]] done! I commend your pains,<br>And everyone shall share i’th’ gains.<br>And now about the cauldron sing<br>Like elves and fairies in a ring,<br>Enchanting all that you put in. |
| *(didascalia)* | Music and a song |
| HECATE | Black spirits and white, red spirits and grey,<br>Mingle, mingle, mingle, you that mingle may. |
| FOURTH [[characters/witch\|WITCH]] | Titty, Tiffin, keep it stiff in;<br>Firedrake, Puckey, make it lucky;<br>Liard, Robin, you must bob in. |
| ALL | Round, around, around, about, about,<br>All ill come running in, all good keep out. |
| FOURTH [[characters/witch\|WITCH]] | Here’s the [[motifs/blood\|blood]] of a bat. |
| HECATE | Put in that, O put in that! |
| FIFTH [[characters/witch\|WITCH]] | Here’s leopard’s bane. |
| HECATE | Put in a grain. |
| FOURTH [[characters/witch\|WITCH]] | The juice of toad, the oil of adder. |
| FIFTH [[characters/witch\|WITCH]] | Those will make the younker madder. |
| HECATE | Put in, there’s all, and rid the stench.<br>A [[characters/witch\|WITCH]]<br>Nay, here’s three ounces of a red-haired wench. |
| ALL | Round, around, around, about, about,<br>All ill come running in, all good keep out. |
| SECOND [[characters/witch\|WITCH]] | By the pricking of my thumbs,<br>Something wicked this way comes. |
| *(didascalia)* | Knock within |
| SECOND [[characters/witch\|WITCH]] | Open, locks, whoever knocks. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/macbeth\|Macbeth]] |
| [[characters/macbeth\|MACBETH]] | How now, you secret, black, and midnight hags,<br>What is’t you do? |
| ALL THE WITCHES | A deed without a name. |
| [[characters/macbeth\|MACBETH]] | I conjure you by that which you profess,<br>Howe’er you come to know it, answer me.<br>Though you untie the winds and let them fight<br>Against the churches, though the yeasty waves<br>Confound and [[motifs/swallow\|swallow]] navigation up,<br>Though bladed corn be lodged and trees blown down,<br>Though castles topple on their warders’ heads,<br>Though palaces and pyramids do slope<br>Their heads to their foundations, though the treasure<br>Of [[concepts/nature\|nature]]’s germens tumble all together<br>Even till destruction sicken, answer me<br>To what I ask you. |
| FIRST [[characters/witch\|WITCH]] | Speak. |
| SECOND [[characters/witch\|WITCH]] | Demand. |
| THIRD [[characters/witch\|WITCH]] | We’ll answer. |
| FIRST [[characters/witch\|WITCH]] | Say if thou’dst rather hear it from our mouths<br>Or from our masters. |
| [[characters/macbeth\|MACBETH]] | Call ‘em, let me see ’em. |
| FIRST [[characters/witch\|WITCH]] | Pour in sow’s [[motifs/blood\|blood]] that hath eaten 80<br>Her nine farrow; grease that’s sweaten<br>From the murderer’s gibbet throw<br>Into the flame. |
| ALL THE WITCHES | Come high or low,<br>Thyself and office deftly show. |
| *(didascalia)* | Thunder. First Apparition: an armed head |
| [[characters/macbeth\|MACBETH]] | Tell me, thou unknown power— |
| FIRST [[characters/witch\|WITCH]] | He knows thy thought.<br>Hear his speech, but say thou naught. |
| FIRST APPARITION | [[characters/macbeth\|Macbeth]], [[characters/macbeth\|Macbeth]], [[characters/macbeth\|Macbeth]], beware [[characters/macduff\|Macduff]],<br>Beware the Thane of Fife. Dismiss me. Enough.<br>Apparition descends |
| [[characters/macbeth\|MACBETH]] | Whate’er thou art, for thy good caution thanks.<br>Thou hast harped my [[concepts/fear\|fear]] aright. But one word<br>more— |
| FIRST [[characters/witch\|WITCH]] | He will not be commanded. Here’s another,<br>More potent than the first. |
| *(didascalia)* | Thunder. Second Apparition: a bloody [[archetypes/child\|child]] |
| SECOND APPARITION | [[characters/macbeth\|Macbeth]], [[characters/macbeth\|Macbeth]], [[characters/macbeth\|Macbeth]]. |
| [[characters/macbeth\|MACBETH]] | Had I three ears I’d hear thee. |
| SECOND APPARITION | Be bloody, bold, and resolute. Laugh to scorn<br>The power of man, for none of woman born<br>Shall harm [[characters/macbeth\|Macbeth]].<br>Apparition descends |
| [[characters/macbeth\|MACBETH]] | Then live, [[characters/macduff\|Macduff]]—what need I [[concepts/fear\|fear]] of thee?<br>But yet I’ll make assurance [[topoi/double\|double]] sure,<br>And take a bond of fate thou shalt not live,<br>That I may tell pale-hearted [[concepts/fear\|fear]] it lies,<br>And [[motifs/sleep\|sleep]] in spite of thunder. |
| *(didascalia)* | Thunder. Third Apparition: a [[archetypes/child\|child]] crowned, with a |
| [[characters/macbeth\|MACBETH]] | tree in his hand<br>What is this<br>That rises like the issue of a [[archetypes/king\|king]],<br>And wears upon his baby-brow the round<br>And top of sovereignty? |
| ALL THE WITCHES | Listen, but speak not to’t. |
| THIRD APPARITION | Be [[motifs/lion\|lion]]-mettled, proud, and take no care<br>Who chafes, who frets, or where conspirers are.<br>[[characters/macbeth\|Macbeth]] shall never vanquished be until<br>Great Birnam Wood to high Dunsinane [[motifs/hill\|Hill]]<br>Shall come against him.<br>Apparition descends |
| [[characters/macbeth\|MACBETH]] | That will never be.<br>Who can impress the forest, bid the tree<br>Unfix his earth-bound root? Sweet bodements, good!<br>Rebellious dead, rise never till the wood<br>Of Birnam rise, and on’s high place [[characters/macbeth\|Macbeth]]<br>Shall live the lease of [[concepts/nature\|nature]], pay his breath<br>To [[motifs/time\|time]] and mortal custom. Yet my heart<br>Throbs to know one thing. Tell me, if your art<br>Can tell so much, shall [[characters/banquo\|Banquo]]’s issue ever<br>Reign in this kingdom? |
| ALL THE WITCHES | Seek to know no more. |
| [[characters/macbeth\|MACBETH]] | I will be satisfied. Deny me this,<br>And an eternal curse fall on you! Let me know.<br>The cauldron sinks. Hautboys<br>Why sinks that cauldron? And what noise is this? |
| FIRST [[characters/witch\|WITCH]] | Show. |
| SECOND [[characters/witch\|WITCH]] | Show. |
| THIRD [[characters/witch\|WITCH]] | Show. |
| ALL THE WITCHES | Show his eyes and grieve his heart,<br>Come like shadows, so depart.<br>A show of eight kings, the last with a glass in his<br><br>hand; and [[characters/banquo\|Banquo]] |
| [[characters/macbeth\|MACBETH]] | Thou art too like the spirit of [[characters/banquo\|Banquo]]. Down!<br>Thy [[motifs/crown\|crown]] does sear mine eyeballs. And thy hair,<br>Thou other gold-bound brow, is like the first.<br>A third is like the former. Filthy hags,<br>Why do you show me this?—A fourth? Start, eyes!<br>What, will the line stretch out to th’ crack of doom?<br>Another yet? A seventh? I’ll see no more—<br>And yet the eighth appears, who bears a glass<br>Which shows me many more; and some I see<br>That twofold balls and treble sceptres carry.<br>Horrible sight! Now I see ’tis true,<br>For the [[motifs/blood\|blood]]-baltered [[characters/banquo\|Banquo]] smiles upon me,<br>And points at them for his. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt kings and [[characters/banquo\|Banquo]] |
| [[characters/macbeth\|MACBETH]] | What, is this so? |
| *(didascalia)* | HECATE |
| [[characters/macbeth\|MACBETH]] | Ay, sir, all this is so. But why<br>Stands [[characters/macbeth\|Macbeth]] thus amazedly?<br>Come, sisters, cheer we up his sprites,<br>And show the best of our delights.<br>I’ll charm the air to give a sound<br>While you perform your antic round,<br>That this great [[archetypes/king\|king]] may kindly say<br>Our duties did his welcome pay. |
| *(didascalia)* | Music. The Witches [[motifs/dance\|dance]], and vanish |
| [[characters/macbeth\|MACBETH]] | Where are they? Gone? Let this pernicious hour<br>Stand aye accursed in the calendar.<br>Come in, without there. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Lennox |
| LENNOX | What’s your grace’s will? |
| [[characters/macbeth\|MACBETH]] | Saw you the weird sisters? |
| LENNOX | No, my lord. |
| [[characters/macbeth\|MACBETH]] | Came they not by you? |
| LENNOX | No, indeed, my lord. |
| [[characters/macbeth\|MACBETH]] | Infected be the air whereon they ride,<br>And damned all those that trust them. I did hear<br>The galloping of [[motifs/horse\|horse]]. Who was’t came by? |
| LENNOX | ’Tis two or three, my lord, that bring you word<br>[[characters/macduff\|Macduff]] is fled to England. |
| [[characters/macbeth\|MACBETH]] | Fled to England? |
| LENNOX | Ay, my good lord. |
| [[characters/macbeth\|MACBETH]] | (aside)<br>[[motifs/time\|Time]], thou anticipat‘st my dread exploits.<br>The flighty purpose never is o’ertook<br>Unless the deed go with it. From this moment<br>The very firstlings of my heart shall be<br>The firstlings of my hand. And even now,<br>To [[motifs/crown\|crown]] my thoughts with acts, be it thought and<br>done: 165<br>The [[settings/castle\|castle]] of [[characters/macduff\|Macduff]] I will surprise,<br>Seize upon Fife, give to th‘edge o’th’ sword<br>His wife, his babes, and all unfortunate souls<br>That trace him in his line. No boasting like a [[archetypes/fool\|fool]];<br>This deed I’ll do before this purpose cool.<br>But no more sights! (To Lennox) Where are these<br>gentlemen?<br>Come bring me where they are. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt |

### Atto 4, Scena 2

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/macduff\|Macduff]]’s Wife, her Son, and Ross |
| [[characters/lady-macduff\|LADY MACDUFF]] | What had he done to make him fly the land? |
| ROSS | You must have patience, madam. |
| [[characters/lady-macduff\|LADY MACDUFF]] | He had none.<br>His flight was [[concepts/madness\|madness]]. When our actions do not,<br>Our fears do make us traitors.<br>Ross<br>You know not<br>Whether it was his wisdom or his [[concepts/fear\|fear]]. |
| [[characters/lady-macduff\|LADY MACDUFF]] | Wisdom—to leave his wife, to leave his babes,<br>His mansion, and his titles in a place<br>From whence himself does fly? He loves us not,<br>He wants the natural touch, for the poor wren,<br>The most diminutive of [[motifs/birds\|birds]], will fight,<br>Her young ones in her nest, against the owl.<br>All is the [[concepts/fear\|fear]] and nothing is the [[motifs/love\|love]];<br>As little is the wisdom, where the flight<br>So runs against all reason.<br>Ross<br>My dearest coz,<br>I pray you school yourself. But for your husband,<br>He is noble, wise, judicious, and best knows<br>The fits o’th’ season. I dare not speak much further,<br>But cruel are the times when we are traitors<br>And do not know ourselves; when we hold rumour<br>From what we [[concepts/fear\|fear]], yet know not what we [[concepts/fear\|fear]],<br>But float upon a wild and violent sea<br>Each way and none. I take my leave of you;<br>Shall not be long but I’ll be here again.<br>Things at the worst will cease, or else climb upward<br>To what they were before. My pretty cousin,<br>Blessing upon you! |
| [[characters/lady-macduff\|LADY MACDUFF]] | Fathered he is, and yet he’s fatherless. |
| ROSS | I am so much a [[archetypes/fool\|fool]], should I stay longer<br>It would be my disgrace and your discomfort.<br>I take my leave at once. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit |
| [[characters/lady-macduff\|LADY MACDUFF]] | Sirrah, your father’s dead,<br>And what will you do now? How will you live? |
| [[characters/macduff\|MACDUFF]]’S SON | As [[motifs/birds\|birds]] do, mother. |
| [[characters/lady-macduff\|LADY MACDUFF]] | What, with worms and flies? |
| [[characters/macduff\|MACDUFF]]’S SON | With what I get, I mean, and so do they. |
| [[characters/lady-macduff\|LADY MACDUFF]] | Poor bird, thou’dst never [[concepts/fear\|fear]] the net nor lime,<br>The pitfall nor the gin. |
| [[characters/macduff\|MACDUFF]]’S SON | Why should I, mother? Poor [[motifs/birds\|birds]] they are not set for.<br>My father is not dead, for all your saying. |
| [[characters/lady-macduff\|LADY MACDUFF]] | Yes, he is dead. How wilt thou do for a father? |
| [[characters/macduff\|MACDUFF]]’S SON | Nay, how will you do for a husband? |
| [[characters/lady-macduff\|LADY MACDUFF]] | Why, I can buy me twenty at any market. |
| [[characters/macduff\|MACDUFF]]’S SON | Then you’ll buy ’em to sell again. |
| [[characters/lady-macduff\|LADY MACDUFF]] | Thou speak‘st with all thy wit, and yet, i’[[concepts/faith\|faith]], with wit enough for thee. |
| [[characters/macduff\|MACDUFF]]’S SON | Was my father a traitor, mother? |
| [[characters/lady-macduff\|LADY MACDUFF]] | Ay, that he was. |
| [[characters/macduff\|MACDUFF]]’S SON | What is a traitor? |
| [[characters/lady-macduff\|LADY MACDUFF]] | Why, one that swears and lies. |
| [[characters/macduff\|MACDUFF]]’S SON | And be all traitors that do so? |
| [[characters/lady-macduff\|LADY MACDUFF]] | Everyone that does so is a traitor, and must be hanged. |
| [[characters/macduff\|MACDUFF]]’S SON | And must they all be hanged that swear and lie? |
| [[characters/lady-macduff\|LADY MACDUFF]] | Every one. |
| [[characters/macduff\|MACDUFF]]’S SON | Who must hang them? |
| [[characters/lady-macduff\|LADY MACDUFF]] | Why, the honest men. |
| [[characters/macduff\|MACDUFF]]’S SON | Then the liars and swearers are fools, for there are liars and swearers enough to beat the honest men and hang up them. |
| [[characters/lady-macduff\|LADY MACDUFF]] | Now God help thee, poor monkey! But how wilt thou do for a father? |
| [[characters/macduff\|MACDUFF]]’S SON | If he were dead you’d weep for him. If you would not, it were a good sign that I should quickly have a new father. |
| [[characters/lady-macduff\|LADY MACDUFF]] | Poor prattler, how thou talk’st! |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter a Messenger |
| MESSENGER | Bless you, fair dame. I am not to you known,<br>Though in your state of [[concepts/honour\|honour]] I am perfect.<br>I [[concepts/doubt\|doubt]] some danger does approach you nearly.<br>If you will take a homely man’s advice,<br>Be not found here. Hence with your little ones!<br>To fright you thus methinks I am too savage,<br>To do worse to you were fell cruelty,<br>Which is too nigh your person. Heaven preserve you.<br>I dare abide no longer. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit Messenger |
| [[characters/lady-macduff\|LADY MACDUFF]] | Whither should I fly?<br>I have done no harm. But I remember now<br>I am in this earthly world, where to do harm<br>Is often laudable, to do good sometime<br>Accounted dangerous folly. Why then, alas,<br>Do I put up that womanly defence<br>To say I have done no harm? |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Murderers |
| [[characters/lady-macduff\|LADY MACDUFF]] | What are these faces?<br>A MURDERER Where is your husband? |
| [[characters/lady-macduff\|LADY MACDUFF]] | I [[concepts/hope\|hope]] in no place so unsanctified<br>Where such as thou mayst find him.<br>A MURDERER<br>He’s a traitor. |
| [[characters/macduff\|MACDUFF]]’S SON | Thou liest, thou shag-haired [[archetypes/villain\|villain]].<br>A MURDERER (stabbing him)<br>What, you egg!<br>Young fry of treachery! |
| [[characters/macduff\|MACDUFF]]’S SON | He has killed me, mother.<br>Run away, I pray you. |
| *(didascalia)* | He dies. Exit [[characters/macduff\|Macduff]]’s Wife crying ‘Murder!’ |
| *(didascalia)* | followed by Murderers with the Son’s [[concepts/body\|body]] |

### Atto 4, Scena 3

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/malcolm\|Malcolm]] and [[characters/macduff\|Macduff]] |
| [[characters/malcolm\|MALCOLM]] | Let us seek out some desolate shade, and there<br>Weep our sad bosoms empty. |
| [[characters/macduff\|MACDUFF]] | Let us rather<br>Hold fast the mortal sword, and like good men<br>Bestride our downfall birthdom. Each new morn<br>New widows howl, new orphans cry, new sorrows<br>Strike heaven on the face that it resounds<br>As if it felt with Scotland and yelled out<br>Like syllable of dolour. |
| [[characters/malcolm\|MALCOLM]] | What I believe I’ll wail,<br>What know believe; and what I can redress,<br>As I shall find the [[motifs/time\|time]] to friend, I will.<br>What you have spoke it may be so, perchance.<br>This [[archetypes/tyrant\|tyrant]], whose sole name blisters our tongues,<br>Was once thought honest. You have loved him [[motifs/well\|well]].<br>He hath not touched you yet. I am young, but<br>something<br>You may discern of him through me: and wisdom<br>To offer up a weak poor [[archetypes/innocent\|innocent]] [[motifs/lamb\|lamb]]<br>T’appease an angry god. |
| [[characters/macduff\|MACDUFF]] | I am not treacherous. |
| [[characters/malcolm\|MALCOLM]] | But [[characters/macbeth\|Macbeth]] is.<br>A good and virtuous [[concepts/nature\|nature]] may recoil<br>In an imperial charge. But I shall crave your pardon.<br>That which you are my thoughts cannot transpose.<br>Angels are bright still, though the brightest fell.<br>Though all things foul would wear the brows of grace,<br>Yet grace must still look so. |
| [[characters/macduff\|MACDUFF]] | I have lost my hopes. |
| [[characters/malcolm\|MALCOLM]] | Perchance even there where I did find my doubts.<br>Why in that rawness left you wife and [[archetypes/child\|child]],<br>Those precious motives, those strong knots of [[motifs/love\|love]],<br>Without leave-taking? I pray you,<br>Let not my jealousies be your dishonours,<br>But mine own safeties. You may be rightly just,<br>Whatever I shall think. |
| [[characters/macduff\|MACDUFF]] | Bleed, bleed, poor country!<br>Great tyranny, lay thou thy basis sure,<br>For goodness dare not check thee. Wear thou thy<br>wrongs;<br>The title is affeered. Fare thee [[motifs/well\|well]], lord.<br>I would not be the [[archetypes/villain\|villain]] that thou think’st<br>For the whole space that’s in the [[archetypes/tyrant\|tyrant]]’s grasp,<br>And the rich east to boot. |
| [[characters/malcolm\|MALCOLM]] | Be not offended.<br>I speak not as in absolute [[concepts/fear\|fear]] of you.<br>I think our country sinks beneath the yoke.<br>It weeps, it bleeds, and each new day a gash<br>Is added to her wounds. I think withal<br>There would be hands uplifted in my right,<br>And here from gracious England have I offer<br>Of goodly thousands. But for all this,<br>When I shall tread upon the [[archetypes/tyrant\|tyrant]]’s head,<br>Or wear it on my sword, yet my poor country<br>Shall have more vices than it had before,<br>More suffer, and more sundry ways, than ever,<br>By him that shall succeed. |
| [[characters/macduff\|MACDUFF]] | What should he be? |
| [[characters/malcolm\|MALCOLM]] | It is myself I mean, in whom I know<br>All the particulars of vice so grafted<br>That when they shall be opened black [[characters/macbeth\|Macbeth]]<br>Will seem as pure as [[motifs/snow\|snow]], and the poor state<br>Esteem him as a [[motifs/lamb\|lamb]], being compared<br>With my confineless harms. |
| [[characters/macduff\|MACDUFF]] | Not in the legions<br>Of horrid hell can come a devil more damned<br>In evils to top [[characters/macbeth\|Macbeth]]. |
| [[characters/malcolm\|MALCOLM]] | I grant him bloody,<br>Luxurious, avaricious, false, deceitful,<br>Sudden, malicious, smacking of every sin<br>That has a name. But there’s no [[characters/bottom\|bottom]], none,<br>In my voluptuousness. Your wives, your daughters,<br>Your matrons, and your maids could not fill up<br>The cistern of my lust, and my [[motifs/desire\|desire]]<br>All continent impediments would o’erbear<br>That did oppose my will. Better [[characters/macbeth\|Macbeth]]<br>Than such an one to reign. |
| [[characters/macduff\|MACDUFF]] | Boundless intemperance<br>In [[concepts/nature\|nature]] is a tyranny. It hath been<br>Th’untimely emptying of the happy throne,<br>And fall of many kings. But [[concepts/fear\|fear]] not yet<br>To take upon you what is yours. You may<br>Convey your pleasures in a spacious plenty<br>And yet seem cold. The [[motifs/time\|time]] you may so hoodwink.<br>We have willing dames enough. There cannot be<br>That vulture in you to devour so many<br>As will to [[concepts/greatness\|greatness]] dedicate themselves,<br>Finding it so inclined. |
| [[characters/malcolm\|MALCOLM]] | With this there grows<br>In my most ill-composed affection such<br>A staunchless avarice that were I [[archetypes/king\|king]]<br>I should cut off the nobles for their lands,<br>[[motifs/desire\|Desire]] his jewels and this other’s house,<br>And my more having would be as a sauce<br>To make me hunger more, that I should forge<br>Quarrels unjust against the good and loyal,<br>Destroying them for wealth. |
| [[characters/macduff\|MACDUFF]] | This avarice<br>Sticks deeper, grows with more pernicious root<br>Than [[motifs/summer\|summer]]-seeming lust, and it hath been<br>The sword of our slain kings. Yet do not [[concepts/fear\|fear]].<br>Scotland hath foisons to fill up your will<br>Of your mere own. All these are portable,<br>With other graces weighed. |
| [[characters/malcolm\|MALCOLM]] | But I have none. The [[archetypes/king\|king]]-becoming graces,<br>As justice, verity, temp’rance, stableness,<br>Bounty, perseverance, [[concepts/mercy\|mercy]], lowliness,<br>Devotion, patience, [[concepts/courage\|courage]], fortitude,<br>I have no relish of them, but abound<br>In the division of each several crime,<br>Acting it many ways. Nay, had I power I should<br>Pour the sweet milk of concord into hell,<br>Uproar the universal peace, confound<br>All unity on earth. |
| [[characters/macduff\|MACDUFF]] | O Scotland, Scotland! |
| [[characters/malcolm\|MALCOLM]] | If such a one be fit to govern, speak.<br>I am as I have spoken. |
| [[characters/macduff\|MACDUFF]] | Fit to govern?<br>No, not to live. O nation miserable,<br>With an untitled [[archetypes/tyrant\|tyrant]] bloody-sceptered,<br>When shalt thou see thy wholesome days again,<br>Since that the truest issue of thy throne<br>By his own interdiction stands accursed<br>And does blaspheme his breed? Thy royal father<br>Was a most sainted [[archetypes/king\|king]]. The [[characters/queen\|Queen]] that bore thee,<br>Oft‘ner upon her knees than on her feet,<br>Died every day she lived. Fare thee [[motifs/well\|well]].<br>These evils thou repeat’st upon thyself<br>Hath banished me from Scotland. O, my breast—<br>Thy [[concepts/hope\|hope]] ends here! |
| [[characters/malcolm\|MALCOLM]] | [[characters/macduff\|Macduff]], this noble passion,<br>[[archetypes/child\|Child]] of integrity, hath from my [[motifs/soul\|soul]]<br>Wiped the black scruples, reconciled my thoughts<br>To thy good [[concepts/truth\|truth]] and [[concepts/honour\|honour]]. Devilish [[characters/macbeth\|Macbeth]]<br>By many of these trains hath sought to win me<br>Into his power, and modest wisdom plucks me<br>From over-credulous haste; but God above<br>Deal between thee and me, for even now<br>I put myself to thy direction and<br>Unspeak mine own detraction, here abjure<br>The taints and blames I laid upon myself<br>For strangers to my [[concepts/nature\|nature]]. I am yet<br>Unknown to woman, never was forsworn,<br>Scarcely have coveted what was mine own,<br>At no [[motifs/time\|time]] broke my [[concepts/faith\|faith]], would not betray<br>The devil to his fellow, and delight<br>No less in [[concepts/truth\|truth]] than life. My first false-speaking<br>Was this upon myself. What I am truly<br>Is thine and my poor country’s to command,<br>Whither indeed, before thy here-approach,<br>Old Siward with ten thousand warlike men,<br>Already at a point, was setting forth.<br>Now we’ll together; and the chance of goodness<br>Be like our warranted quarrel!—Why are you silent? |
| [[characters/macduff\|MACDUFF]] | Such welcome and unwelcome things at once<br>’is hard to reconcile. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter a Doctor |
| [[characters/malcolm\|MALCOLM]] | [[motifs/well\|Well]], more anon. (To the Doctor) Comes the [[archetypes/king\|King]]<br>forth, I pray you? |
| DOCTOR | Ay, sir. There are a crew of wretched souls<br>That stay his cure. Their malady convinces<br>The great [[forms/essay\|essay]] of art, but at his touch,<br>Such sanctity hath Heaven given his hand,<br>They presently amend. |
| [[characters/malcolm\|MALCOLM]] | I thank you, doctor. Exit Doctor |
| [[characters/macduff\|MACDUFF]] | What’s the disease he means? |
| [[characters/malcolm\|MALCOLM]] | ’is called the evil—<br>A most miraculous work in this good [[archetypes/king\|King]],<br>Which often since my here-remain in England<br>I have seen him do. How he solicits heaven<br>Himself best knows, but strangely visited people,<br>All swoll’n and ulcerous, pitiful to the eye,<br>The mere [[concepts/despair\|despair]] of surgery, he cures,<br>Hanging a golden stamp about their necks,<br>Put on with holy prayers; and ’is spoken,<br>To the succeeding royalty he [[motifs/leaves\|leaves]]<br>The healing benediction. With this strange virtue<br>He hath a heavenly gift of prophecy,<br>And sundry blessings hang about his throne<br>That speak him full of grace. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Ross |
| [[characters/macduff\|MACDUFF]] | See who comes here. |
| [[characters/malcolm\|MALCOLM]] | My countryman, but yet I know him not. |
| [[characters/macduff\|MACDUFF]] | My ever gentle cousin, welcome hither. |
| [[characters/malcolm\|MALCOLM]] | I know him now. Good God betimes remove<br>The means that makes us strangers!<br>Ross<br>Sir, amen. |
| [[characters/macduff\|MACDUFF]] | Stands Scotland where it did?<br>Ross<br>Alas, poor country,<br>Almost afraid to know itself. It cannot<br>Be called our mother, but our [[motifs/grave\|grave]], where nothing<br>But who knows nothing is once seen to smile;<br>Where sighs and groans and shrieks that rend the air<br>Are made, not marked; where violent sorrow seems<br>A modern ecstasy. The dead man’s knell<br>Is there scarce asked for who, and good men’s lives<br>Expire before the [[motifs/flowers\|flowers]] in their caps,<br>Dying or ere they sicken. |
| [[characters/macduff\|MACDUFF]] | O relation<br>Too nice and yet too true! |
| [[characters/malcolm\|MALCOLM]] | What’s the newest grief? |
| ROSS | That of an hour’s age doth hiss the speaker;<br>Each minute teems a new one. |
| [[characters/macduff\|MACDUFF]] | How does my wife? |
| ROSS | Why, [[motifs/well\|well]]. |
| [[characters/macduff\|MACDUFF]] | And all my children?<br>Ross<br>[[motifs/well\|Well]], too. |
| [[characters/macduff\|MACDUFF]] | The [[archetypes/tyrant\|tyrant]] has not battered at their peace? |
| ROSS | No, they were [[motifs/well\|well]] at peace when I did leave ’em. |
| [[characters/macduff\|MACDUFF]] | Be not a niggard of your speech. How goes’t? |
| ROSS | When I came hither to transport the tidings<br>Which I have heavily borne, there ran a rumour<br>Of many worthy fellows that were out,<br>Which was to my belief witnessed the rather<br>For that I saw the [[archetypes/tyrant\|tyrant]]’s power afoot.<br>Now is the [[motifs/time\|time]] of help. (To [[characters/malcolm\|Malcolm]]) Your eye in<br>Scotland<br>Would create soldiers, make our women fight<br>To doff their dire distresses. |
| [[characters/malcolm\|MALCOLM]] | Be’t their comfort<br>We are coming thither. Gracious England hath<br>Lent us good Siward and ten thousand men;<br>An older and a better soldier none<br>That Christendom gives out.<br>Ross<br>Would I could answer<br>This comfort with the like. But I have words<br>That would be howled out in the [[motifs/desert\|desert]] air<br>Where hearing should not latch them. |
| [[characters/macduff\|MACDUFF]] | What concern they—<br>The general cause, or is it a fee-grief<br>Due to some single breast?<br>Ross<br>No mind that’s honest<br>But in it shares some woe, though the main part<br>Pertains to you alone. |
| [[characters/macduff\|MACDUFF]] | If it be mine,<br>Keep it not from me; quickly let me have it. |
| ROSS | Let not your ears despise my tongue for ever,<br>Which shall possess them with the heaviest sound<br>That ever yet they heard. |
| [[characters/macduff\|MACDUFF]] | H’m, I guess at it. |
| ROSS | Your [[settings/castle\|castle]] is surprised, your wife and babes<br>Savagely slaughtered. To relate the manner<br>Were on the quarry of these murdered deer<br>To add the [[motifs/death\|death]] of you. |
| [[characters/malcolm\|MALCOLM]] | Merciful heaven!<br>(To [[characters/macduff\|Macduff]]) What, man, ne’er pull your hat upon<br>your brows.<br>Give sorrow words. The grief that does not speak<br>Whispers the o’erfraught heart and bids it break. |
| [[characters/macduff\|MACDUFF]] | My children too?<br>Ross<br>Wife, children, servants, all<br>That could be found. |
| [[characters/macduff\|MACDUFF]] | And I must be from thence!<br>My wife killed too? |
| ROSS | I have said. |
| [[characters/malcolm\|MALCOLM]] | Be comforted.<br>Let’s make us medicines of our great [[concepts/revenge\|revenge]]<br>To cure this deadly grief. |
| [[characters/macduff\|MACDUFF]] | He has no children. All my pretty ones?<br>Did you say all? O hell-kite! All?<br>What, all my pretty chickens and their dam<br>At one fell swoop? |
| [[characters/malcolm\|MALCOLM]] | Dispute it like a man. |
| [[characters/macduff\|MACDUFF]] | I shall do so,<br>But I must also feel it as a man.<br>I cannot but remember such things were<br>That were most precious to me. Did heaven look on<br>And would not take their part? Sinful [[characters/macduff\|Macduff]],<br>They were all struck for thee. Naught that I am,<br>Not for their own demerits but for mine<br>Fell slaughter on their souls. Heaven rest them now. |
| [[characters/malcolm\|MALCOLM]] | Be this the whetstone of your sword. Let grief<br>Convert to anger: blunt not the heart, enrage it. |
| [[characters/macduff\|MACDUFF]] | O, I could play the woman with mine eyes<br>And braggart with my tongue! But gentle heavens<br>Cut short all intermission. Front to front<br>Bring thou this fiend of Scotland and myself. |
| *(didascalia)* | Within my sword’s length set him. If he scape, |
| [[characters/macduff\|MACDUFF]] | Heaven forgive him too. |
| [[characters/malcolm\|MALCOLM]] | This tune goes manly.<br>Come, go we to the [[archetypes/king\|King]]. Our power is ready;<br>Our lack is nothing but our leave. [[characters/macbeth\|Macbeth]]<br>Is ripe for shaking, and the powers above<br>Put on their instruments. Receive what cheer you may:<br>The [[motifs/night\|night]] is long that never finds the day. Exeunt |

### Atto 5, Scena 1

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter a Doctor of Physic and a Waiting-Gentlewoman |
| DOCTOR | I have two nights watched with you, but can perceive no [[concepts/truth\|truth]] in your report. When was it she last walked? |
| GENTLEWOMAN | Since his majesty went into the field I have seen her rise from her bed, throw her nightgown upon her, unlock her closet, take forth paper, fold it, write upon’t, read it, afterwards seal it, and again return to bed, yet all this while in a most fast [[motifs/sleep\|sleep]]. |
| DOCTOR | A great perturbation in [[concepts/nature\|nature]], to receive at once the benefit of [[motifs/sleep\|sleep]] and do the effects of watching. In this slumbery agitation besides her walking and other actual performances, what at any [[motifs/time\|time]] have you heard her say? |
| GENTLEWOMAN | That, sir, which I will not report after her. |
| DOCTOR | You may to me; and ’tis most meet you should. |
| GENTLEWOMAN | Neither to you nor anyone, having no witness to confirm my speech. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/lady-macbeth\|Lady Macbeth]] with a taper |
| GENTLEWOMAN | Lo you, here she comes. This is her very guise, and,<br>upon my life, fast asleep. Observe her. Stand close. |
| DOCTOR | How came she by that light? |
| GENTLEWOMAN | Why, it stood by her. She has light by her continually. ’Tis her command. |
| DOCTOR | You see her eyes are open. |
| GENTLEWOMAN | Ay, but their sense are shut. |
| DOCTOR | What is it she does now? Look how she rubs her hands. |
| GENTLEWOMAN | It is an accustomed action with her, to seem thus washing her hands. I have known her continue in this a quarter of an hour. |
| [[characters/lady-macbeth\|LADY MACBETH]] | Yet here’s a spot. |
| DOCTOR | Hark, she speaks. I will set down what comes from her to satisfy my remembrance the more strongly. |
| [[characters/lady-macbeth\|LADY MACBETH]] | Out, damned spot; out, I say. One, two,—why, then ’tis [[motifs/time\|time]] to do’t. Hell is murky. Fie, my lord, fie, a soldier and afeard? What need we [[concepts/fear\|fear]] who knows it when none can call our power to account? Yet who would have thought the old man to have had so much [[motifs/blood\|blood]] in him? |
| DOCTOR | Do you mark that? |
| [[characters/lady-macbeth\|LADY MACBETH]] | The Thane of Fife had a wife. Where is she now? What, will these hands ne’er be clean? No more o’ that, my lord, no more o’ that. You mar all with this starting. |
| DOCTOR | Go to, go to. You have known what you should not. |
| GENTLEWOMAN | She has spoke what she should not, I am sure of that. Heaven knows what she has known. |
| [[characters/lady-macbeth\|LADY MACBETH]] | Here’s the smell of the [[motifs/blood\|blood]] still. All the perfumes of Arabia will not sweeten this little hand. O, O, O! |
| DOCTOR | What a sigh is therel The heart is sorely charged. |
| GENTLEWOMAN | I would not have such a heart in my bosom for the dignity of the whole [[concepts/body\|body]]. |
| DOCTOR | [[motifs/well\|Well]], [[motifs/well\|well]], [[motifs/well\|well]]. |
| GENTLEWOMAN | Pray God it be, sir. |
| DOCTOR | This disease is beyond my practice. Yet I have known those which have walked in their [[motifs/sleep\|sleep]] who have died holily in their beds. |
| [[characters/lady-macbeth\|LADY MACBETH]] | Wash your hands, put on your nightgown, look not so pale. I tell you yet again, [[characters/banquo\|Banquo]]’s buried. He cannot come out on’s [[motifs/grave\|grave]]. |
| DOCTOR | Even so? |
| [[characters/lady-macbeth\|LADY MACBETH]] | To bed, to bed. There’s knocking at the gate. Come, come, come, come, give me your hand. What’s done cannot be undone. To bed, to bed, to bed. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit |
| DOCTOR | Will she go now to bed? |
| GENTLEWOMAN | Directly. |
| DOCTOR | Foul whisp’rings are abroad. Unnatural deeds<br>Do breed unnatural troubles; infected minds<br>To their deaf pillows will discharge their [[motifs/secrets\|secrets]].<br>More needs she the divine than the physician.<br>God, God forgive us all! Look after her.<br>Remove from her the means of all annoyance,<br>And still keep eyes upon her. So, good [[motifs/night\|night]].<br>My mind she has mated, and amazed my sight.<br>I think, but dare not speak. |
| GENTLEWOMAN | Good [[motifs/night\|night]], good doctor. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt |

### Atto 5, Scena 2

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Menteith, Caithness, Angus, Lennox, soldiers, with a drummer and colours |
| MENTEITH | The English power is near, led on by [[characters/malcolm\|Malcolm]],<br>His uncle Siward, and the good [[characters/macduff\|Macduff]].<br>Revenges burn in them, for their dear causes<br>Would to the bleeding and the grim alarm<br>Excite the mortified man. |
| ANGUS | Near Birnam Wood<br>Shall we [[motifs/well\|well]] meet them. That way are they coming. |
| CAITHNESS | Who knows if Donalbain be with his brother? |
| LENNOX | For certain, sir, he is not. I have a file<br>Of all the gentry. There is Siward’s son,<br>And many unrough youths that even now 10<br>Protest their first of manhood. |
| MENTEITH | What does the [[archetypes/tyrant\|tyrant]]? |
| CAITHNESS | Great Dunsinane he strongly fortifies.<br>Some say he’s mad, others that lesser hate him<br>Do call it valiant fury; but for certain<br>He cannot buckle his distempered cause |
| *(didascalia)* | Within the belt of rule. |
| ANGUS | Now does he feel<br>His secret murders sticking on his hands.<br>Now minutely revolts upbraid his [[concepts/faith\|faith]]-breach.<br>Those he commands move only in command,<br>Nothing in [[motifs/love\|love]]. Now does he feel his title<br>Hang loose about him, like a giant’s robe<br>Upon a dwarfish thief. |
| MENTEITH | Who then shall blame<br>His pestered senses to recoil and start<br>When all that is within him does condemn<br>Itself for being there? |
| CAITHNESS | [[motifs/well\|Well]], march we on<br>To give obedience where ’tis truly owed.<br>Meet we the medicine of the sickly weal,<br>And with him pour we in our country’s purge,<br>Each drop of us. |
| LENNOX | Or so much as it needs<br>To dew the sovereign flower and drown the weeds.<br>Make we our march towards Birnam. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt, marching |

### Atto 5, Scena 3

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/macbeth\|Macbeth]], the Doctor of Physic, and attendants |
| [[characters/macbeth\|MACBETH]] | Bring me no more reports. Let them fly all.<br>Till Birnam Wood remove to Dunsinane<br>I cannot taint with [[concepts/fear\|fear]]. What’s the boy [[characters/malcolm\|Malcolm]]?<br>Was he not born of woman? The spirits that know<br>All mortal consequences have pronounced me thus:<br>‘[[concepts/fear\|Fear]] not, [[characters/macbeth\|Macbeth]]. No man that’s born of woman<br>Shall e’er have power upon thee.’ Then fly, false thanes,<br>And mingle with the English epicures.<br>The mind I sway by and the heart I bear<br>Shall never sag with [[concepts/doubt\|doubt]] nor shake with [[concepts/fear\|fear]]. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Servant |
| [[characters/macbeth\|MACBETH]] | The devil damn thee black, thou cream-faced loon!<br>Where gott’st thou that goose look? |
| SERVANT | There is ten thousand- |
| [[characters/macbeth\|MACBETH]] | Geese, [[archetypes/villain\|villain]]? |
| SERVANT | Soldiers, sir. 15 |
| [[characters/macbeth\|MACBETH]] | Go prick thy face and over-red thy [[concepts/fear\|fear]],<br>Thou lily-livered boy. What soldiers, patch?<br>[[motifs/death\|Death]] of thy [[motifs/soul\|soul]], those linen cheeks of thine<br>Are counsellors to [[concepts/fear\|fear]]. What soldiers, whey-face? |
| SERVANT | The English force, so please you. |
| [[characters/macbeth\|MACBETH]] | Take thy face hence. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit Servant |
| [[characters/macbeth\|MACBETH]] | Seyton!-I am sick at heart<br>When I behold-Seyton, I say!—This push<br>Will cheer me ever or disseat me now.<br>I have lived long enough. My way of life<br>Is fall’n into the sere, the yellow leaf,<br>And that which should accompany old age,<br>As [[concepts/honour\|honour]], [[motifs/love\|love]], obedience, troops of friends,<br>I must not look to have, but in their stead<br>Curses, not loud but deep, mouth-[[concepts/honour\|honour]], breath<br>Which the poor heart would fain deny and dare not.<br>Seyton! |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Seyton |
| SEYTON | What’s your gracious pleasure? |
| [[characters/macbeth\|MACBETH]] | What news more? |
| SEYTON | All is confirmed, my lord, which was reported. |
| [[characters/macbeth\|MACBETH]] | I’ll fight till from my bones my flesh be hacked.<br>Give me my armour. |
| SEYTON | ’Tis not needed yet. |
| [[characters/macbeth\|MACBETH]] | I’ll put it on.<br>Send out more horses. Skirr the country round.<br>Hang those that talk of [[concepts/fear\|fear]]. Give me mine armour.<br>How does your patient, doctor? |
| DOCTOR | Not so sick, my lord,<br>As she is troubled with thick-coming fancies<br>That keep her from her rest. |
| [[characters/macbeth\|MACBETH]] | Cure her of that.<br>Canst thou not minister to a mind diseased,<br>Pluck from the [[motifs/memory\|memory]] a rooted sorrow,<br>Raze out the written troubles of the brain,<br>And with some sweet oblivious antidote<br>Cleanse the fraught bosom of that perilous stuff<br>Which weighs upon the heart? |
| DOCTOR | Therein the patient<br>Must minister to himself. |
| [[characters/macbeth\|MACBETH]] | Throw physic to the dogs; I’ll none of it.<br>(To an attendant) Come, put mine armour on. Give me<br>my staff.<br>Seyton, send out. Doctor, the thanes fly from me.<br>(To an attendant) Come, sir, dispatch.—If thou couldst,<br>doctor, cast<br>The [[motifs/water\|water]] of my land, find her disease,<br>And purge it to a sound and pristine health,<br>I would applaud thee to the very echo,<br>That should applaud again. (To an attendant) Pull’t off,<br>I say.<br>(To the Doctor) What rhubarb, cyme, or what<br>purgative drug<br>Would scour these English hence? Hear’st thou of<br>them? |
| DOCTOR | Ay, my good lord. Your royal preparation<br>Makes us hear something. |
| [[characters/macbeth\|MACBETH]] | (To an attendant) Bring it after me.<br>I will not be afraid of [[motifs/death\|death]] and bane<br>Till Birnam Forest come to Dunsinane. |
| DOCTOR | (aside)<br>Were I from Dunsinane away and clear,<br>Profit again should hardly draw me here. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt |

### Atto 5, Scena 4

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/malcolm\|Malcolm]], Siward, [[characters/macduff\|Macduff]], Siward’s Son, Menteith, Caithness, Angus, and soldiers, marching, with a drummer and colours |
| [[characters/malcolm\|MALCOLM]] | Cousins, I [[concepts/hope\|hope]] the days are near at hand<br>That chambers will be safe. |
| MENTEITH | We [[concepts/doubt\|doubt]] it nothing. |
| SIWARD | What wood is this before us? |
| MENTEITH | The wood of Birnam. |
| [[characters/malcolm\|MALCOLM]] | Let every soldier hew him down a bough<br>And bear’t before him. Thereby shall we [[archetypes/shadow\|shadow]]<br>The numbers of our host, and make discovery<br>Err in report of us.<br>A SOLDIER<br>It shall be done. |
| SIWARD | We learn no other but the confident [[archetypes/tyrant\|tyrant]]<br>Keeps still in Dunsinane, and will endure<br>Our setting down before’t. |
| [[characters/malcolm\|MALCOLM]] | ’Tis his main [[concepts/hope\|hope]],<br>For where there is advantage to be gone,<br>Both more and less have given him the revolt,<br>And none serve with him but constrained things,<br>Whose hearts are absent too. |
| [[characters/macduff\|MACDUFF]] | Let our just censures<br>Attend the true event, and put we on<br>Industrious soldiership. |
| SIWARD | The [[motifs/time\|time]] approaches<br>That will with due decision make us know<br>What we shall say we have, and what we owe.<br>Thoughts speculative their unsure hopes relate,<br>But certain issue strokes must arbitrate;<br>Towards which, advance the war. Exeunt, marching |

### Atto 5, Scena 5

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/macbeth\|Macbeth]], Seyton, and soldiers, with a drummer and colours |
| [[characters/macbeth\|MACBETH]] | Hang out our banners on the outward walls.<br>The cry is still ‘They come.’ Our [[settings/castle\|castle]]’s strength<br>Will laugh a siege to scorn. Here let them lie<br>Till famine and the ague eat them up.<br>Were they not forced with those that should be ours<br>We might have met them dareful, beard to beard,<br>And beat them backward [[motifs/home\|home]].<br>A cry within of women<br>What is that noise? |
| SEYTON | It is the cry of women, my good lord.<br>[Exit] |
| [[characters/macbeth\|MACBETH]] | I have almost forgot the taste of fears.<br>The [[motifs/time\|time]] has been my senses would have cooled<br>To hear a [[motifs/night\|night]]-shriek, and my fell of hair<br>Would at a dismal treatise rouse and stir<br>As life were in’t. I have supped full with horrors.<br>Direness, familiar to my slaughterous thoughts,<br>Cannot once start me. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Seyton |
| [[characters/macbeth\|MACBETH]] | Wherefore was that cry? |
| SEYTON | The [[characters/queen\|Queen]], my lord, is dead. |
| [[characters/macbeth\|MACBETH]] | She should have died hereafter.<br>There would have been a [[motifs/time\|time]] for such a word.<br>Tomorrow, and tomorrow, and tomorrow<br>Creeps in this petty pace from day to day<br>To the last syllable of recorded [[motifs/time\|time]],<br>And all our yesterdays have lighted fools<br>The way to dusty [[motifs/death\|death]]. Out, out, brief candle.<br>Life’s but a walking [[archetypes/shadow\|shadow]], a poor player<br>That struts and frets his hour upon the stage,<br>And then is heard no more. It is a tale<br>Told by an idiot, full of sound and fury,<br>Signifying nothing. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter a Messenger |
| [[characters/macbeth\|MACBETH]] | Thou com’st to use<br>Thy tongue: thy story quickly. |
| MESSENGER | Gracious my lord,<br>I should report that which I say I saw,<br>But know not how to do’t. |
| [[characters/macbeth\|MACBETH]] | [[motifs/well\|Well]], say, sir. |
| MESSENGER | As I did stand my watch upon the [[motifs/hill\|hill]]<br>I looked toward Birnam, and anon methought<br>The wood began to move. |
| [[characters/macbeth\|MACBETH]] | Liar and slave! |
| MESSENGER | Let me endure your wrath if’t be not so. |
| *(didascalia)* | Within this three mile may you see it coming. |
| MESSENGER | I say, a moving grove. |
| [[characters/macbeth\|MACBETH]] | If thou speak’st false<br>Upon the next tree shall thou hang alive<br>Till famine cling thee. If thy speech be sooth,<br>I care not if thou dost for me as much.<br>I pall in resolution, and begin<br>To [[concepts/doubt\|doubt]] th‘equivocation of the fiend,<br>That lies like [[concepts/truth\|truth]]. ’[[concepts/fear\|Fear]] not till Birnam Wood<br>Do come to Dunsinane‘—and now a wood<br>Comes toward Dunsinane. Arm, arm, and out.<br>If this which he avouches does appear<br>There is nor flying hence nor tarrying here.<br>I ’gin to be aweary of the sun,<br>And wish th‘estate o’th’ world were now undone.<br>Ring the alarum bell. Alarums Blow [[motifs/wind\|wind]], come wrack,<br>At least we’ll die with harness on our back. Exeunt |

### Atto 5, Scena 6

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/malcolm\|Malcolm]], Siward, [[characters/macduff\|Macduff]], and their army with boughs, with a drummer and colours |
| [[characters/malcolm\|MALCOLM]] | Now near enough. Your leafy screens throw down,<br>And show like those you are.<br>They throw down the boughs<br>You, worthy uncle,<br>Shall with my cousin, your right noble son,<br>Lead our first battle. Worthy [[characters/macduff\|Macduff]] and we<br>Shall take upon’s what else remains to do<br>According to our order. |
| SIWARD | Fare you [[motifs/well\|well]].<br>Do we but find the [[archetypes/tyrant\|tyrant]]’s power tonight,<br>Let us be beaten if we cannot fight. |
| [[characters/macduff\|MACDUFF]] | Make all our trumpets speak, give them all breath,<br>Those clamorous harbingers of [[motifs/blood\|blood]] and [[motifs/death\|death]]. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt. Alarums continued |

### Atto 5, Scena 7

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/macbeth\|Macbeth]] |
| [[characters/macbeth\|MACBETH]] | They have tied me to a stake. I cannot fly,<br>But bear-like I must fight the course. What’s he<br>That was not born of woman? Such a one<br>Am I to [[concepts/fear\|fear]], or none. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Young Siward |
| YOUNG SIWARD | What is thy name? |
| [[characters/macbeth\|MACBETH]] | Thou’lt be afraid to hear it. |
| YOUNG SIWARD | No, though thou call’st thyself a hotter name<br>Than any is in hell. |
| [[characters/macbeth\|MACBETH]] | My name’s [[characters/macbeth\|Macbeth]]. |
| YOUNG SIWARD | The devil himself could not pronounce a title<br>More hateful to mine ear. |
| [[characters/macbeth\|MACBETH]] | No, nor more fearful. |
| YOUNG SIWARD | Thou liest, abhorred [[archetypes/tyrant\|tyrant]]. With my sword<br>I’ll prove the lie thou speak’st.<br>They fight, and Young Siward is slain |
| [[characters/macbeth\|MACBETH]] | Thou wast born of woman,<br>But swords I smile at, weapons laugh to scorn,<br>Brandished by man that’s of a woman born. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit with the [[concepts/body\|body]] |

### Atto 5, Scena 8

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Alarums. Enter [[characters/macduff\|Macduff]] |
| [[characters/macduff\|MACDUFF]] | That way the noise is. [[archetypes/tyrant\|Tyrant]], show thy face!<br>If thou beest slain and with no stroke of mine,<br>My wife and children’s [[motifs/ghosts\|ghosts]] will haunt me still.<br>I cannot strike at wretched kerns, whose arms<br>Are hired to bear their staves. Either thou, [[characters/macbeth\|Macbeth]], 5<br>Or else my sword with an unbattered edge<br>I sheathe again undeeded. There thou shouldst be;<br>By this great clatter one of greatest note<br>Seems bruited. Let me find him, fortune,<br>And more I beg not. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit. Alarums |

### Atto 5, Scena 9

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/malcolm\|Malcolm]] and Siward |
| SIWARD | This way, my lord. The [[settings/castle\|castle]]’s gently rendered.<br>The [[archetypes/tyrant\|tyrant]]’s people on both sides do fight.<br>The noble thanes do bravely in the war.<br>The day almost itself professes yours,<br>And little is to do. |
| [[characters/malcolm\|MALCOLM]] | We have met with foes<br>That strike beside us. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter, sir, the [[settings/castle\|castle]]. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt. Alarum |

### Atto 5, Scena 10

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/macbeth\|Macbeth]] |
| [[characters/macbeth\|MACBETH]] | Why should I play the Roman [[archetypes/fool\|fool]], and die<br>On mine own sword? Whiles I see lives, the gashes<br>Do better upon them. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/macduff\|Macduff]] |
| [[characters/macduff\|MACDUFF]] | Turn, hell-hound, turn. |
| [[characters/macbeth\|MACBETH]] | Of all men else I have avoided thee.<br>But get thee back. My [[motifs/soul\|soul]] is too much charged<br>With [[motifs/blood\|blood]] of thine already. |
| [[characters/macduff\|MACDUFF]] | I have no words;<br>My voice is in my sword, thou bloodier [[archetypes/villain\|villain]]<br>Than terms can give thee out.<br>They fight; alarum |
| [[characters/macbeth\|MACBETH]] | Thou losest labour.<br>As easy mayst thou the intrenchant air<br>With thy keen sword impress as make me bleed.<br>Let fall thy blade on vulnerable crests;<br>I bear a charmed life, which must not yield<br>To one of woman born. |
| [[characters/macduff\|MACDUFF]] | [[concepts/despair\|Despair]] thy charm,<br>And let the angel whom thou still hast served<br>Tell thee [[characters/macduff\|Macduff]] was from his mother’s womb 15<br>Untimely ripped. |
| [[characters/macbeth\|MACBETH]] | Accursèd be that tongue that tells me so,<br>For it hath cowed my better part of man;<br>And be these juggling fiends no more believed,<br>That palter with us in a [[topoi/double\|double]] sense,<br>That keep the word of promise to our ear<br>And break it to our [[concepts/hope\|hope]]. I’ll not fight with thee. |
| [[characters/macduff\|MACDUFF]] | Then yield thee, coward,<br>And live to be the show and gaze o‘th’ [[motifs/time\|time]].<br>We’ll have thee as our rarer monsters are,<br>Painted upon a pole, and underwrit<br>‘Here may you see the [[archetypes/tyrant\|tyrant]].’ |
| [[characters/macbeth\|MACBETH]] | I will not yield<br>To kiss the ground before young [[characters/malcolm\|Malcolm]]’s feet,<br>And to be baited with the rabble’s curse.<br>Though Birnam Wood be come to Dunsinane,<br>And thou opposed being of no woman born,<br>Yet I will try the last. Before my [[concepts/body\|body]]<br>I throw my warlike shield. Lay on, [[characters/macduff\|Macduff]],<br>And damned be him that first cries ‘Hold, enough!’ |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt fighting. Alarums |
| [[characters/macbeth\|MACBETH]] | They enter fighting, and [[characters/macbeth\|Macbeth]] is slain. Exit<br><br>[[characters/macduff\|Macduff]] with [[characters/macbeth\|Macbeth]]’s [[concepts/body\|body]] |

### Atto 5, Scena 11

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Retreat and flourish. Enter with a drummer and colours [[characters/malcolm\|Malcolm]], Siward, Ross, thanes, and soldiers |
| [[characters/malcolm\|MALCOLM]] | I would the friends we miss were safe arrived. |
| SIWARD | Some must go off; and yet by these I see<br>So great a day as this is cheaply bought. |
| [[characters/malcolm\|MALCOLM]] | [[characters/macduff\|Macduff]] is missing, and your noble son. |
| ROSS | (to Siward)<br>Your son, my lord, has paid a soldier’s debt.<br>He only lived but till he was a man,<br>The which no sooner had his prowess confirmed<br>In the unshrinking station where he fought,<br>But like a man he died. |
| SIWARD | Then he is dead? |
| ROSS | Ay, and brought off the field. Your cause of sorrow<br>Must not be measured by his worth, for then<br>It hath no end. |
| SIWARD | Had he his hurts before? |
| ROSS | Ay, on the front. |
| SIWARD | Why then, God’s soldier be he.<br>Had I as many sons as I have hairs<br>I would not wish them to a fairer [[motifs/death\|death]];<br>And so his knell is knolled. |
| [[characters/malcolm\|MALCOLM]] | He’s worth more sorrow,<br>And that I’ll spend for him. |
| SIWARD | He’s worth no more.<br>They say he parted [[motifs/well\|well]] and paid his score,<br>And so God be with him. Here comes newer comfort. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/macduff\|Macduff]] with [[characters/macbeth\|Macbeth]]’s head |
| [[characters/macduff\|MACDUFF]] | (to [[characters/malcolm\|Malcolm]])<br>Hail, [[archetypes/king\|King]], for so thou art. Behold where stands<br>Th’[[archetypes/usurper\|usurper]]’s cursed head. The [[motifs/time\|time]] is free.<br>I see thee compassed with thy kingdom’s pearl,<br>That speak my salutation in their minds,<br>Whose voices I [[motifs/desire\|desire]] aloud with mine:<br>Hail, [[archetypes/king\|King]] of Scotland! |
| ALL BUT [[characters/malcolm\|MALCOLM]] | Hail, [[archetypes/king\|King]] of Scotland! |
| *(didascalia)* | Flourish |
| [[characters/malcolm\|MALCOLM]] | We shall not spend a large expense of [[motifs/time\|time]]<br>Before we reckon with your several loves<br>And make us even with you. My thanes and kinsmen,<br>Henceforth be earls, the first that ever Scotland<br>In such an [[concepts/honour\|honour]] named. What’s more to do<br>Which would be planted newly with the [[motifs/time\|time]],<br>As calling [[motifs/home\|home]] our exiled friends abroad,<br>That fled the snares of watchful tyranny,<br>Producing forth the cruel ministers<br>Of this dead butcher and his fiend-like [[characters/queen\|queen]]-<br>Who, as ‘tis thought, by [[concepts/self\|self]] and violent hands<br>Took off her life—this and what needful else<br>That calls upon us, by the grace of grace<br>We will perform in measure, [[motifs/time\|time]], and place.<br>So thanks to all at once, and to each one,<br>Whom we invite to see us crowned at Scone. |
| *(didascalia)* | Flourish. Exeunt Omnes |
| FIRST | printed in the 1623 Folio, Antony and [[characters/cleopatra\|Cleopatra]] had been entered on the Stationers’ Register on 20 May 1608. Echoes of it in Barnabe Barnes’s [[forms/tragedy\|tragedy]] The Devil’s Charter, acted by Shakespeare’s company in February 1607, suggest that Shakespeare wrote his play no later than 1606, and stylistic evidence supports that date.<br>The Life of Marcus Antonius in Sir Thomas North’s [[forms/translation\|translation]] of Plutarch’s Lives of the Noble Grecians and Romans (1579) was one of the sources for [[characters/julius-caesar\|Julius Caesar]]; it also provided Shakespeare with most of his material for Antony and [[characters/cleopatra\|Cleopatra]], in which he draws upon its language to a remarkable extent even in some of the play’s most poetic passages. For example, [[characters/enobarbus\|Enobarbus]]’ famous description of [[characters/cleopatra\|Cleopatra]] in her barge (2.2.197-225) incorporates phrase after phrase of North’s prose. And the play’s action stays close to North’s account, though with significant adjustments, particularly compressions of the [[motifs/time\|time]]-scheme. It opens in 40 BC, two years after the end of [[characters/julius-caesar\|Julius Caesar]], and portrays events that took place over a period of ten years. [[characters/mark-antony\|Mark Antony]] has become an older man, though Octavius is still ‘scarce-bearded’. Plutarch, who was a connoisseur of human behaviour, also afforded many hints for the characterization; but some characters, particularly Antony’s comrade Domitius [[characters/enobarbus\|Enobarbus]] and [[characters/cleopatra\|Cleopatra]]’s women, [[characters/charmian\|Charmian]] and Iras, are largely created by Shakespeare.<br>In the earlier play, [[characters/mark-antony\|Mark Antony]] had formed a triumvirate with [[characters/octavius-caesar\|Octavius Caesar]] and [[characters/lepidus\|Lepidus]]. In Antony and [[characters/cleopatra\|Cleopatra]] the triumvirate is in a state of disintegration, partly because [[characters/mark-antony\|Mark Antony]]—married at the play’s opening to Fulvia, who is rebelling against [[characters/octavius-caesar\|Octavius Caesar]]—is infatuated with [[characters/cleopatra\|Cleopatra]], [[characters/queen\|Queen]] of [[settings/egypt\|Egypt]] (and the former mistress of [[characters/julius-caesar\|Julius Caesar]]). The play’s action swings between Rome and Alexandria as Antony is torn between the claims of Rome—strengthened for a while by his [[motifs/marriage\|marriage]], after Fulvia’s [[motifs/death\|death]], to [[characters/octavius-caesar\|Octavius Caesar]]’s sister [[characters/octavia\|Octavia]]—and the temptations of [[settings/egypt\|Egypt]]. Gradually opposition between Antony and Octavius increases, until they engage in a sea-fight near Actium (in Greece), in which Antony follows [[characters/cleopatra\|Cleopatra]]’s navy in ignominious retreat. The closing stages of the [[topoi/double\|double]] [[forms/tragedy\|tragedy]] portray Antony’s shame, humiliation, and [[concepts/suicide\|suicide]] after [[characters/cleopatra\|Cleopatra]] falsely causes him to believe that she has killed herself; faced with the threat that Caesar will take her captive to Rome, [[characters/cleopatra\|Cleopatra]] too commits [[concepts/suicide\|suicide]]. According to Plutarch, she was thirty-eight years old; as for Antony, ‘some say that he lived three-and-fifty years, and others say, six-and-fifty’.<br>In Antony and [[characters/cleopatra\|Cleopatra]] the classical restraint of [[characters/julius-caesar\|Julius Caesar]] gives way to a fine excess of language, of dramatic action, and of individual behaviour. The style is hyperbolical, overflowing the measure of the iambic pentameter. The action is amazingly fluid, shifting with an ease and rapidity that caused bewilderment to ages unfamiliar with the conventions of Shakespeare’s theatre. And the characterization is correspondingly extravagant, delighting in the quirks of individual behaviour, above all in the paradoxes and inconsistencies of the Egyptian [[characters/queen\|queen]] who contains within herself the capacity for every extreme of human behaviour, from [[concepts/vanity\|vanity]], meanness, and frivolity to the [[concepts/sublime\|sublime]] [[concepts/self\|self]]-transcendence with which she faces and embraces [[motifs/death\|death]]. |
| THE PERSONS OF THE | PLAY |


<span class="qlang-split" data-lang="it"></span>

[[characters/macbeth|MACBETH]]
BY WILLIAM SHAKESPEARE (ADAPTED BY THOMAS MIDDLETON)
SHORTLY after James VI of Scotland succeeded to the English throne, in 1603, he gave his patronage to Shakespeare’s company; the Lord Chamberlain’s Men became the [[archetypes/king|King]]’s Men, entering into a special relationship with their sovereign. [[characters/macbeth|Macbeth]] is the play of Shakespeare’s that most clearly reflects this relationship. James regarded the virtuous and noble [[characters/banquo|Banquo]], [[characters/macbeth|Macbeth]]’s comrade at the start of the action, as his direct ancestor; eight Stuart kings were said to have preceded James, just as, in the play, [[characters/banquo|Banquo]] points to ‘a show of eight kings’ as his descendants (4.1.127.1-140); and in the play the English [[archetypes/king|king]] (historically Edward the Confessor) is praised for the capacity, on which James also prided himself, to cure ’the [[archetypes/king|king]]’s evil’ (scrofula). [[characters/macbeth|Macbeth]] is obviously a Jacobean play, composed probably in 1606.
But the first printed text, in the 1623 Folio, shows signs of having been adapted at a later date. It is exceptionally short by comparison with Shakespeare’s other tragedies; and it includes episodes which there is good reason to believe are not by Shakespeare. Most conspicuous are Act 3, Scene 5 and parts of Act 4, Scene I: 38.I-60 and 141-8.1. These episodes feature Hecate, who does not appear elsewhere in the play; they are composed largely in octosyllabic couplets in a style conspicuously different from the rest of the play; and they call for the performance of two songs that are found in The [[characters/witch|Witch]], a play of uncertain date by Thomas Middleton. Probably Middleton himself adapted Shakespeare’s play some years after its first performance, adding these and more localized details, and cutting the play elsewhere. We do not attempt to excise passages most clearly written by Middleton, because the adapter’s hand almost certainly affected the text at other, less determinable points. The Folio text of [[characters/macbeth|Macbeth]] cites only the opening words of the songs; drawing on The [[characters/witch|Witch]], we attempt a reconstruction of their staging in [[characters/macbeth|Macbeth]].
Shakespeare took materials for his story from the account in Raphael Holinshed’s Chronicle of the reigns of [[characters/duncan|Duncan]] and [[characters/macbeth|Macbeth]] (AD 1034-57). Occasionally (especially in the English episodes of Act 4, Scene 2) he closely followed Holinshed’s wording, but essentially the play’s structure is his own. He invented the framework of the [[characters/three-witches|three witches]] who tempted both [[characters/macbeth|Macbeth]] and [[characters/banquo|Banquo]] with prophecies of [[concepts/greatness|greatness]]. His [[characters/macbeth|Macbeth]] is both more introspective and more intensely evil than the competent warrior-[[archetypes/king|king]] portrayed by Holinshed; conversely, Shakespeare made [[characters/duncan|Duncan]], the [[archetypes/king|king]] whom [[characters/macbeth|Macbeth]] murders, far more venerable and saintly. Some of the play’s features, notably the character of [[characters/lady-macbeth|Lady Macbeth]], originate in Holinshed’s account of the murder of an earlier Scottish [[archetypes/king|king]], Duff; he was killed in his [[settings/castle|castle]] at Forres by Donwald, who had been ‘set on’ by his wife.
[[characters/macbeth|Macbeth]] is an exciting story of witchcraft, murder, and retribution that can also be seen as a study in the philosophy and psychology of evil. The witches are not easily made credible in modern performances, and Shakespeare seems deliberately to have drained colour away from some parts of his composition in order to concentrate attention on [[characters/macbeth|Macbeth]] and his Lady. It is [[characters/macbeth|Macbeth]]’s neurotic [[concepts/self|self]]-absorption, his [[concepts/fear|fear]], his anger, and his [[concepts/despair|despair]], along with his wife’s steely determination, her invoking of the powers of evil, and her eventual revelation in [[motifs/sleep|sleep]] of her repressed humanity, that have given the play its long-proven power to fascinate readers and to challenge performers.
THE PERSONS OF THE PLAY
[[archetypes/king|KING]] [[characters/duncan|DUNCAN]] of Scotland
A CAPTAIN in [[characters/duncan|Duncan]]’s army
[[characters/macbeth|MACBETH]], Thane of Glamis, later Thane of Cawdor, then [[archetypes/king|King]] of
Scotland
A PORTER at [[characters/macbeth|Macbeth]]’s [[settings/castle|castle]]
Three MURDERERS attending on [[characters/macbeth|Macbeth]]
SEYTON, servant of [[characters/macbeth|Macbeth]]
[[characters/lady-macbeth|LADY MACBETH]], [[characters/macbeth|Macbeth]]’s wife
[[characters/banquo|BANQUO]], a Scottish thane
FLEANCE, his son
[[characters/macduff|MACDUFF]], Thane of Fife
[[characters/lady-macduff|LADY MACDUFF]], his wife
